 succeeded in 241ms:
\section{Fibonacci prime-power realization}
\label{sec:prime-power-realization}

The witness-cover classification is structural.  We now supply the
Fibonacci-specific input that determines which prime powers can occur in its
atomic families.  These formulas will be used explicitly in the upper bounds.

\begin{proposition}[Classical prime-power rank lifting]
\label{prop:prime-power-ladders}
Let $p$ be prime, and put $z_p:=\alpha(p)$. For $p\ne2,5$, put
$h_p:=\nu_p(F_{z_p})$. Then
\[
\alpha(2)=3,\qquad \alpha(4)=6,\qquad
\alpha(2^e)=3\cdot2^{e-2}\quad(e\ge3),
\]
\[
\alpha(5^e)=5^e\quad(e\ge1),
\]
and, for $p\ne2,5$,
\[
\alpha(p^e)=p^{\max\{e-h_p,0\}}z_p\quad(e\ge1).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Wall's rank-divisibility theorem gives
\[
p\mid F_r\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad z_p\mid r
\]
\cite{Wall1960FibonacciModm}. For $p\ne2,5$, the classical congruence
\[
F_{p-(5/p)}\equiv0\pmod p
\]
gives $z_p\mid p-(5/p)$, so $p\nmid z_p$. Lengyel's valuation formula
\cite[Theorems~1 and~2]{Lengyel1995OrderFibonacciLucas}, in the treatment of
Medina--Rowland \cite{MedinaRowland2015PRegularity}, states that when
$z_p\mid r$,
\[
\nu_p(F_r)=\nu_p(F_{z_p})+\nu_p(r).
\]
Writing $r=z_pu$ yields
$\nu_p(F_{z_pu})=h_p+\nu_p(u)$. Thus $p^e\mid F_{z_pu}$ if and only if
$\nu_p(u)\ge e-h_p$, and the least admissible $u$ is
$p^{\max\{e-h_p,0\}}$.

For the exceptional primes, the same valuation formulas specialize to
\[
\nu_5(F_r)=\nu_5(r),
\qquad
\nu_2(F_r)=
\begin{cases}
0, & 3\nmid r,\\
1, & r\equiv3\pmod 6,\\
3, & r\equiv6\pmod {12},\\
\nu_2(r)+2, & 12\mid r.
\end{cases}
\]
The least index divisible by $5^e$ is $5^e$. For $2$, the least indices
with valuation at least $1$, $2$, and $3$ are $3$, $6$, and $6$; for
$e\ge4$ the least admissible multiple of $12$ is $3\cdot2^{e-2}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Atomic prime-power classification]
\label{cor:atomic-classification}
The atomic prime powers are exactly the following:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $2\in\Atom(3)$, $4\in\Atom(6)$, and
$2^e\in\Atom(3\cdot2^{e-2})$ for every $e\ge4$; the power $8$ is not
atomic.
\item $5^e\in\Atom(5^e)$ for every $e\ge1$.
\item If $p\ne2,5$, $z_p=\alpha(p)$, and
$h_p=\nu_p(F_{z_p})$, then $p\in\Atom(z_p)$ and
$p^e\in\Atom(p^{e-h_p}z_p)$ for every $e>h_p$; no power $p^e$ with
$1<e\le h_p$ is atomic.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently every atom is either a prime atom or a ladder atom $p^e$ with
$e>1$. Every ladder step changes only the $p$-adic coordinate of its
apparition index when it is lowered to $p^{e-1}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-power-ladders}. For $p=2$, the ranks are
$3,6,6,12,24,\ldots$ on $2,4,8,16,32,\ldots$. For $p=5$, every step is
atomic. If $p\ne2,5$, the rank remains $z_p$ for $1\le e\le h_p$ and then
gains one factor of $p$ at each further level. The last assertion follows
from the same formulas.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Prime-ladder dichotomy]
\label{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy}
Let $\theta=p^e\in\Atom(d)$ and suppose $d\mid n$. If $e=1$, then
\[
E_n(\theta)=T_n(\theta).
\]
If $e>1$, then $E_n(\theta)$ is either empty or the singleton $\{p\}$.
In particular, no Fibonacci atom has
\[
1<\#E_n(\theta)<\#T_n(\theta).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $e=1$, then $d^-(\theta)=\alpha(1)=1$, so every coordinate in
$T_n(\theta)$ is lost after lowering. Assume $e>1$. By
Corollary~\ref{cor:atomic-classification}, $\theta$ is a ladder atom.
Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-power-ladders} shows that $d(\theta)$ and
$d^-(\theta)$ differ only in their $p$-adic coordinate. Therefore the only
full coordinate that can be lost is $p$ itself, and the result follows.
\end{proof}

For nonempty $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, define the prime window
\[
P_J(n):=\coprod_{\substack{d\mid n\\T_n(d)=J}}\Pi_\alpha(d),
\]
where $T_n(d)=\{\ell\in\mathcal P(n):
\nu_\ell(d)=\nu_\ell(n)\}$.  A prime in $P_J(n)$ is identified with its
prime atom.

\begin{proposition}[Atomic families and unique ladder slots]
\label{prop:atomic-family-ladder-slots}
Let $n\ge3$ and let $\varnothing\ne I\subseteq J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$.
Then every atom in $\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)$ is either in the prime window
$P_J(n)$, which occurs only when $I=J$, or in a ladder family with
$I=\{p\}$ for a prime coordinate $p\in J$.  Each such ladder family contains
at most one atom.

More precisely, put $a=\nu_p(n)$.  Its only possible ladder atom is
\[
\lambda_p(n)=
\begin{cases}
4, & p=2,\ a=1,\\
2^{a+2}, & p=2,\ a\ge2,\\
5^a, & p=5,\ a\ge2,\\
p^{h_p+a}, & p\notin\{2,5\},
\end{cases}
\]
with no candidate for $p=5$, $a=1$.  The candidate belongs to
$\mathcal A_{\{p\},J}(n)$ exactly when
$d(\lambda_p(n))\mid n$ and $T_n(\lambda_p(n))=J$.
Consequently every atomic family is finite, every nonsingleton atomic family
is a finite disjoint union of exact-rank prime classes, and every labelled
ladder slot has at most one candidate.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If $\theta=q$ is a prime atom, the prime-ladder dichotomy gives
$E_n(q)=T_n(q)$, and $q$ belongs to the unique class
$\Pi_\alpha(d(q))$ in $P_{T_n(q)}(n)$. Conversely every prime in $P_J(n)$
has $(E_n,T_n)=(J,J)$.

Suppose $\theta=q^e$ is a ladder atom. The same dichotomy gives
$E_n(\theta)=\{q\}$ whenever the essential support is nonempty. Hence its
essential coordinate determines its underlying prime, so $q=p$. The
full-exponent equation
$\nu_p(d(p^e))=\nu_p(n)=a$ and
Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-power-ladders} now leave only the displayed
candidate. For $p=2$, the atomic ladder levels are $4$ of rank $6$ and
$2^e$ of rank $3\cdot2^{e-2}$ for $e\ge4$. For $p=5$, the rank is $5^e$,
and $e=1$ is a prime atom. For $p\notin\{2,5\}$, one has
$p\nmid z_p$ and
$\alpha(p^e)=p^{e-h_p}z_p$ at atomic ladder levels, so $e=h_p+a$.
The remaining conditions $d(\theta)\mid n$ and $T_n(\theta)=J$ are exactly
the conditions for membership in $\mathcal A_{\{p\},J}(n)$.

Finally, each $\Pi_\alpha(d)$ is finite by
Lemma~\ref{lem:primitive-prime-mobius-count}, and there are only finitely many
divisors $d$ of $n$. This proves all finiteness and uniqueness assertions.
\end{proof}


 succeeded in 247ms:
\begin{theorem}[Multiplicity-free support entropy with arithmetic remainder]
\label{thm:minimal-generator-growth}
For every integer $n\ge3$, put $k=\omega(n)$ and
$L(n)=\log\#\mathcal M_n$. Then $\mathcal M_n$ is nonempty. If $k\ge3$ and
$r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}
 (2^r-1)^{k-r}
 \le \#\mathcal M_n
 \le \sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
\end{equation}
In particular, uniformly along every sequence for which $\omega(n)\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}
 \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2-O(1)
 \le L(n)
 \le \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2
      +\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n)).
\end{equation}
No exact quadratic coefficient for the full quantity $L(n)$ is claimed.
The coefficient $\log2/4$ is exact only for the multiplicity-free
minimal-cover contribution: the lower construction embeds the unweighted
cover count, and the private-coordinate encoding gives the same quadratic
support term in the upper bound. The display is not an asymptotic formula for
$L(n)$ unless $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$; no such unconditional estimate for
$R(n)$ is proved here. In particular, the displayed coefficient is not
claimed to be optimal among all deductions from fibotomic or exact-rank
arithmetic information.

For comparison, the crude support-count estimate gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}
 \#\mathcal M_n
 \le \sum_{r=0}^{k}\binom{\Omega(F_n)}{r}
 \le n^k.
\end{equation}
Combining only the last inequality with the classical maximal order of
$\omega(n)$ gives the crude corollary
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:crude-maximal-order}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{L(n)\log\log n}{(\log n)^2}\le1.
\end{equation}
This envelope has scale $(\log n)^2/\log\log n$, a factor $\log\log n$
above the witness-cover entropy scale; it is not a consequence of
\eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
At the coarser divisor-lattice scale, the antichain property and Wigert's
theorem give the Sperner-type envelope
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}
 L(n)\le\log\tau(F_n)
 \le (\log2\,\log\varphi+o(1))\frac{n}{\log n},
 \qquad \varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2};
\end{equation}
in fact \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} is stronger and implies
$L(n)=o(n/\log n)$.

Moreover, as $x\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}
 \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)x(\log\log x)^2
 \le \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 \le (1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
\end{equation}

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First, $F_n\in B_n$ for every $n\ge3$.  Indeed,
$F_n\mid F_d$ implies $F_n\le F_d$, and strict increase of the Fibonacci
sequence from index $2$ onward forces $d\ge n$; hence $\alpha(F_n)=n$.
Because $B_n$ is a nonempty finite poset, it has a minimal element, so
$\mathcal M_n\ne\varnothing$ and $L(n)$ is defined.

We first prove the explicit finite private-cover bound in
\eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}. Fix $k\ge3$, and give
$\mathcal P(n)$ an arbitrary order. Put
\[
 X=\mathcal P(n)\cap\{2,3\},\qquad r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor.
\]
Since $k-\#X\ge k-2\ge r$, choose an $r$-element set
$P\subseteq\mathcal P(n)\setminus X$, and put
$Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. For every map
\[
 H:Q\longrightarrow 2^P\setminus\{\varnothing\}
\]
and every $i\in P$, define
\[
 T_i=\{i\}\cup\{q\in Q:i\in H(q)\}.
\]
The index $n_{T_i}=\prod_{\ell\in T_i}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)}$ has a prime
divisor outside $\{2,3\}$, because $i\in P$. Hence $n_{T_i}\ge5$ and
$n_{T_i}\notin\{6,12\}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors} supplies a
prime $\pi_{T_i}$ with $\alpha(\pi_{T_i})=n_{T_i}$; fix one such prime for
each support that occurs.

The supports $T_i$ cover $\mathcal P(n)$ because $H(q)$ is nonempty for every
$q\in Q$. Moreover, $i$ belongs to $T_i$ and to no $T_j$ with $j\ne i$.
Each $\pi_{T_i}$ is a prime atom, so
$E_n(\pi_{T_i})=T_n(\pi_{T_i})=T_i$. Thus
$\{\pi_{T_i}:i\in P\}$ is an $n$-witness cover. Distinct supports have
distinct ranks and therefore distinct underlying primes. If two maps $H$
differ, then their support families differ, since the edge containing the
private coordinate $i$ identifies $T_i$; unique factorization then gives
different minimal generators. There are
$(2^r-1)^{\#Q}=(2^r-1)^{k-r}$ maps $H$, proving the lower bound in
\eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.

For the upper bound, let $\Theta$ be an $n$-witness cover with $s$ atoms. For
each $\theta\in\Theta$, choose the least coordinate
\[
 x_\theta\in E_n(\theta)\setminus
 \bigcup_{\eta\in\Theta\setminus\{\theta\}}T_n(\eta).
\]
These private coordinates are distinct; write
$P=\{x_\theta:\theta\in\Theta\}$ and
$Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. If $x_\theta=x$, then
\[
 T_n(\theta)=\{x\}\cup U_\theta,\qquad U_\theta\subseteq Q,
\]
because every other member of $P$ is private to another atom. By the
prime--ladder alternative in Lemma~\ref{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy}, either
$E_n(\theta)=T_n(\theta)$ or $E_n(\theta)=\{x\}$. Hence there are at most two
support-pair alternatives, and the definition
\eqref{eq:rank-window-multiplicity} allows at most $R(n)$ atoms for each.
Thus, once $P$ is fixed, the atom indexed by each
$x\in P$ has at most $2R(n)2^{k-s}$ choices. Ignoring coverage and
coprimeness only enlarges the count, so summing over the
$\binom{k}{s}$ possible sets $P$ and over $1\le s\le k$ proves the upper
bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.

Now $r(k-r)=\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor$ and
$(k-r)\log(1-2^{-r})=O(1)$. Hence the logarithm of the lower bound is
$(\log2)k^2/4+O(1)$. For the upper bound, use
$s(k-s)\le k^2/4$, $s\le k$, and
$\sum_s\binom{k}{s}\le2^k$ to obtain
\[
 \log\sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}
 \le\frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log R(n)+O(k).
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.

Let
\[
 \mathcal Q_n=\{p^e:p\mid F_n,\ 1\le e\le\nu_p(F_n)\}.
\]
This set has cardinality $\Omega(F_n)$.  By unique factorization, the map
\[
 m\longmapsto\{p^{\nu_p(m)}:p\mid m\}
\]
is injective from $\mathcal M_n$ into the subsets of $\mathcal Q_n$.
Corollary~\ref{cor:support-bound} says that every image has at most
$k=\omega(n)$ members.  This proves the first inequality in
\eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.

Since every prime factor of $F_n$ is at least $2$,
$2^{\Omega(F_n)}\le F_n$.  Also $F_n<2^{n-1}$ for $n\ge3$, by the elementary
induction $F_{j+1}=F_j+F_{j-1}<2^j$.  Hence
$\Omega(F_n)\le n-2$.  A subset of an $N$-element set having at most $k$
members can be encoded injectively by its increasing list padded to length
$k$ with a new symbol; therefore
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^k\binom Nr\le(N+1)^k.
\]
Taking $N=\Omega(F_n)$ gives the second inequality in
\eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.

The classical maximal-order formula for $\omega(n)$
\cite[Theorem~22.11]{HardyWright2008Introduction}
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{\omega(n)\log\log n}{\log n}=1
\]
now combines with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$ to prove
\eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order}. This argument uses only the crude estimate
$\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, not the witness-cover entropy bound. On
the other hand,
$\mathcal M_n$ is an antichain in the divisor lattice of $F_n$, so
\[
 \#\mathcal M_n\le\widthop(\operatorname{Div}(F_n))\le\tau(F_n).
\]
Wigert's maximal-order theorem \cite{Wigert1907Divisors} states that
\[
 \log\tau(N)\le(\log2+o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}.
\]
Binet's formula gives $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$ and
$\log\log F_n=\log n+O(1)$, yielding
\eqref{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}.  Finally,
$(\log n)^2/\log\log n=o(n/\log n)$, so
\eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} gives the stated strict improvement.

For the mean upper bound, use the classical mean-value estimate
\cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:mean-omega-input}
 \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=x\log\log x+O(x),
\end{equation}
together with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$.  Uniformly for $n\le x$,
$\log n\le\log x$, and hence
\[
 \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 \le\log x\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)
 =(1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
\]

Tur\'an's second-moment form of the Hardy--Ramanujan theorem
\cite{Turan1934HardyRamanujan} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:omega-second-moment}
 \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)^2=(1+o(1))x(\log\log x)^2.
\end{equation}
After enlarging the absolute constant in the lower half of
\eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, it holds as
$L(n)\ge(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4-O(1)$ also for $\omega(n)<3$.
Summation and \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment} prove the lower half of
\eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth} and complete the proof.
\end{proof}


\begin{definition}[Sufficient maximum-window hypothesis H1]
\label{hyp:normal-window}
For every $\varepsilon>0$,
\[
 \#\{n\le x:\log R(n)>\varepsilon\omega(n)\}=o(x).
\tag{H1}
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Sufficient maximum-window hypothesis H2]
\label{hyp:mean-window}
One has
\[
 \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
 =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
\tag{H2}
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional obstruction from Fibonacci prime divisors]
\label{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window}
Assume Conjecture~5.1 of Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
\cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors}, namely that
$\omega(F_n)\gg\log n$ for composite $n$. Then, for every fixed
$\varepsilon$ with $0<\varepsilon<1-\log2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:blms-normal-window-obstruction}
 \liminf_{x\to\infty}\frac1x
 \#\{n\le x:\log R(n)>\varepsilon\omega(n)\}
 \ge\frac6{\pi^2},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}
 \liminf_{x\to\infty}
 \frac{\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)}
 {x(\log\log x)^2}
 \ge\frac6{\pi^2}(1-\log2)>0.
\end{equation}
In particular, under that published conjecture, both sufficient
maximum-window hypotheses \textup{(H1)} and \textup{(H2)} are false.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $n$ be squarefree and put $k=\omega(n)$. The exact-rank partition of the
prime divisors of $F_n$ gives
\[
 \omega(F_n)=\sum_{d\mid n}a(d).
\]
For squarefree $n$, every nonempty support $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$ has the
single rank $d=\prod_{\ell\in J}\ell$ in its window. Since $a(1)=0$, the
pigeonhole principle and \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole}
 R(n)\ge\max_{d\mid n}a(d)
 \ge\frac{\omega(F_n)}{2^k-1}.
\end{equation}
Under the stated conjecture, for every sufficiently large squarefree composite
$n$,
\[
 \log R(n)\ge\log\log n-k\log2+O(1).
\]
The Hardy--Ramanujan theorem has an exceptional set of $o(x)$ integers, so on
all but $o(x)$ squarefree integers one has
$k=(1+o(1))\log\log n$. Consequently
\[
 \omega(n)\log R(n)
 \ge(1-\log2+o(1))(\log\log n)^2
\]
on this set. Squarefree integers have density $6/\pi^2$, and squarefree prime
indices have density zero. Moreover,
\[
 \frac{\log R(n)}{\omega(n)}\ge1-\log2+o(1)
\]
on the same set. This proves
\eqref{eq:blms-normal-window-obstruction}; summation over squarefree composite
indices proves \eqref{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Purely conditional consequences of H1 and H2]
\label{cor:conditional-orders}
Assuming hypothesis \textup{(H1)} in Definition~\ref{hyp:normal-window},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-normal-order}
 L(n)\sim\frac{\log2}{4}(\log\log n)^2
 \quad\text{for almost all }n.
\end{equation}
Assuming hypothesis \textup{(H2)} in Definition~\ref{hyp:mean-window},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-mean-order}
 \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 \sim\frac{\log2}{4}x(\log\log x)^2.
\end{equation}
These are implications only: neither conclusion is unconditional, and no
positive arithmetic evidence for either sufficient hypothesis is presently
known. Proposition~\ref{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window} shows that the
published Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek conjecture predicts that both
hypotheses fail.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For the first assertion, intersect the density-one set in
Definition~\ref{hyp:normal-window} with the Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order set.
Then the error $\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n))$ in
\eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} is $o(\omega(n)^2)$. For the second,
sum the upper bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, use
Definition~\ref{hyp:mean-window}, \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment}, and
$\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=O(x\log\log x)$, and compare with the unconditional
lower mean bound \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Exact residual arithmetic interface]
\label{rem:exact-average-open-interface}
Supplementary Corollary~\ref{supp-cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} determine the contribution of the
multiplicity-free minimal-cover model used here: $\log2/4$ is its exact
quadratic coefficient, the fixed-choice rank-pure sector supplies its
theta-refined lower terms, and the private-coordinate upper bound retains the
term $\omega(n)\log R(n)$. Proposition~
\ref{prop:atomic-family-ladder-slots} gives the rank-window description for
every support size:
prime-atom families are finite disjoint unions of the rank
classes $\Pi_\alpha(d)$, while ladder families are singletons. Thus
Hypotheses \textup{(H1)} and \textup{(H2)} in Definitions~
\ref{hyp:normal-window} and \ref{hyp:mean-window} ask precisely for sufficient
upper distributional control of
exact-rank prime multiplicities in those rank windows.

The pointwise estimate \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} proves only the
consequence obtained by combining the fibotomic size identity with the
divisibility of the exact-rank radical and the congruences
$p\equiv\pm1\pmod d$. Supplementary Remark~
\ref{supp-rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient} shows that even its
Euler-factor scale does not by itself imply either maximum-window hypothesis. The
manuscript does not prove that these inputs exhaust the information available
from fibotomic arithmetic, or that
\eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy} is optimal within any information class. Such
an optimality theorem would require, at minimum, a precise axiom class of
admissible information and a realizable extremal model satisfying those
axioms. Neither is supplied here. Whether stronger unconditional dispersion or
concentration consequences follow from fibotomic arithmetic is therefore an
open interface.

The closest distribution theorems do not close this interface.  In the
notation of a nondegenerate Lucas sequence $U$ with nonsquare discriminant
$\Delta$ and squarefree discriminant part $\Delta_0$, Sanna's Theorem~1.1
\cite{Sanna2022RankDivisibility} gives, unconditionally, an asymptotic formula
for
\[
 \#\{p\le x:d\mid\rho_U(p)\}
\]
when $d$ is odd, with $3\nmid d$ if $\Delta_0=-3$, and
$x>\exp(Be^{8\omega(d)}d^8)$.  The condition counted is
$d\mid\rho_U(p)$, not the exact-rank condition $\rho_U(p)=d$; for example,
$3\mid\alpha(17)=9$ although $\alpha(17)\ne3$.  Sanna's Theorem~1.1 on the
index of appearance \cite{Sanna2024IndexAppearance} instead assumes GRH and,
for a fixed positive integer $t$, counts primes having
$\iota_U(p)=t$ uniformly for $x>t^3$. Cera Da Concei\c c\~ao removes the
restrictions in the fixed rank-divisibility density problem and gives
explicit densities for all Lucas sequences and fixed $d$
\cite{CeraDaConceicao2026PrimeDensities}; the condition remains
$d\mid\rho_U(p)$ and still does not isolate a growing exact fiber. In the
Fibonacci case,
$\alpha(p)=d$ and $\iota_F(p)=t$ imply
\[
 p=td+\left(\frac5p\right)=td\pm1.
\]
Taking $x$ on the scale of this candidate prime restricts that theorem to
$t^2\ll d$, and its cumulative fixed-index count still does not isolate the
single rank $d$. Thus none of these results controls the growing exact fibers
$\Pi_\alpha(d)$ required in \eqref{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}.

The primitive-divisor theorems of Carmichael and Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier give
existence in the Fibonacci setting. Granville proves an odd-multiplicity
primitive-factor theorem for a class of second-order Lucas recurrences with
$c\equiv2\pmod4$ and explicitly leaves the odd-$b,c$ case, including the
Fibonacci recurrence $b=c=1$, outside that theorem
\cite{Granville2012PrimitivePrimeFactors}. Thus it is a neighboring
Lucas-sequence result, not a Fibonacci multiplicity input here. Kiss treats
related Lucas-sequence primitive-divisor questions
\cite{Kiss1988PrimitiveDivisors}. Hong proves that, for each fixed positive
integer $\kappa$ and all sufficiently large $n$ with an explicit threshold,
$F_n$ has a primitive divisor at least $(\kappa+1)n-1$
\cite{Hong2025BigPrimitiveDivisors}. These results concern existence, parity of
one primitive factor's multiplicity in the stated Lucas class, or the size of
one Fibonacci primitive divisor; they do not provide the required average
dispersion or critical concentration estimates for Fibonacci exact-rank prime
multiplicities in visible divisor windows. The
Hardy--Ramanujan and
Tur\'an theorems control the additive function $\omega(n)$ but not the prime
factorization of $F_d$ across exact apparition ranks. Likewise, the
Erd\H{o}s--Wintner theorem \cite{ErdosWintner1939Additive} concerns limiting
distributions of additive arithmetic functions, whereas $L(n)$ and $R(n)$
are not additive. No implication from RH or ERH to either rank-window
conjecture is presently known. Accordingly, the normal and mean equivalents
\eqref{eq:conditional-normal-order} and
\eqref{eq:conditional-mean-order} are conditional; the unconditional
statements are \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} and
\eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}, together with the stronger connected lower
bound in Supplementary Corollary~
\ref{supp-cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy}.
None of these estimates implies that the disconnected part of $\mathcal M_n$
is negligible; the strictly stronger blockwise hypothesis needed for that
conclusion is isolated in Supplementary Theorem~
\ref{supp-thm:blockwise-connected-dominance}.
\end{remark}

# Reproducing the finite computations

This directory is the computational supplement for the manuscript. All
integer arithmetic is exact. The verification was run with:

- Python 3.10.11;
- SymPy 1.13.1;
- SymPy's `factorint` integer-factorization routine.

The factorization cost is not part of any theorem or complexity claim. The
archive `fibonacci_factorizations_2_210.tsv` records every exact factorization
of `F_n` used for `2 <= n <= 210`; the verifier checks that every row
multiplies back to the stated Fibonacci number.

## Files

- `compute_birth_layer_table.py`: exhaustive divisor/rank enumeration for
  `2 <= n <= 30` and generation of the TeX table.
- `verify_finite_claims.py`: independent direct and structural computations,
  finite-bound checks, exact minimal-cover and connected-cover enumeration,
  theta-normalized and discrete local-limit checks, rank-pure sector checks,
  exact total and connected support-spectrum checks and the extremal
  atomic-product count,
  rank-window deaggregation and squarefree pigeonhole checks, complete
  weighted rank-pure product membership and the strict ladder example at
  `n=91`, refined
  private-coordinate upper bounds, fibotomic rank-entropy and exact-rank
  radical checks, the finite Jarden consequence, exact-rank prime existence
  through rank 210, a prime inverse-ray prefix, the exceptional path to the
  fixed point 12, environment reporting, and
  factorization-archive generation and validation.
- `test_verify_finite_claims.py`: unit tests for the arithmetic routines,
  finite bounds, archive round trip, and report contents.
- `../scripts/verify_deepening_delta.py`: independent legacy verification
  battery for the finite upper-fiber and growth-law checks.
- `../scripts/test_verify_deepening_delta.py`: unit tests for the independent
  legacy verification battery.
- `deepening_delta_verification.txt`: complete deterministic report from the
  independent legacy verification battery.
- `../scripts/verify_squarefree_slice.py`: independent enumeration of
  weighted minimal covers and rank-pure prime products for the canonical
  squarefree exact-fiber slice.
- `../scripts/test_verify_squarefree_slice.py`: unit tests for the sharp
  fixed-total-weight bound, its unique equality profile for `k >= 3` and
  nonuniqueness at `k = 2`, support incidence, and the squarefree-slice set
  equality.
- `squarefree_slice_verification.txt`: complete report for the new sharpness
  and squarefree-fiber checks.
- `named_problem_audit.md`: primary-source quotations, current-status checks,
  and exact mappings from five printed open problems to the manuscript.
- `fibonacci_factorizations_2_210.tsv`: exact factorization input/archive.
- `tab_birth_layer_data.tex`: generated table included in the manuscript.
- `birth_layer_table_output.txt`: complete stdout from table generation.
- `finite_verification.txt`: complete stdout report from the verification run.
- `../.gitattributes`: LF line-ending policy for the reproducibility files.
- `SHA256SUMS`: SHA-256 digest of the LF-stable reproducibility sources,
  archives, reports, documentation, and line-ending policy named in the
  manifest. The generated TeX table is represented by its generator and
  deterministic text transcript rather than hashed directly.

## Commands

Run these commands from the manuscript directory:

```powershell
python -m unittest discover -s artifacts -p "test_verify_finite_claims.py" -v
Push-Location scripts; python -m unittest -v test_verify_squarefree_slice.py; Pop-Location
python artifacts\compute_birth_layer_table.py
python artifacts\verify_finite_claims.py --exhaustive-max 60 --scalable-max 210 --output artifacts\finite_verification.txt --factorizations-output artifacts\fibonacci_factorizations_2_210.tsv
python scripts\verify_deepening_delta.py --exhaustive-max 60 --scalable-max 210 --output artifacts\deepening_delta_verification.txt --factorizations-output artifacts\fibonacci_factorizations_2_210.tsv
python scripts\verify_squarefree_slice.py --max-index 210 --output artifacts\squarefree_slice_verification.txt
```

The first verification route enumerates every divisor of `F_n`, computes its
rank directly, and minimizes the resulting birth layer. On `2 <= n <= 60`,
the second route constructs the upper fiber from the maximal proper Fibonacci
divisors and constructs the minimal elements from the witness threshold
conditions. The report compares the actual sets, not only their cardinalities:

```text
B_n direct = B_n upper fiber: 59/59 set equalities
M_n direct = M_n witness: 59/59 set equalities
```

For `61 <= n <= 210`, the structural route checks the stated finite upper and
lower bounds. On every layer `3 <= n <= 210`, it also enumerates the rank-pure
sector (all layers in this range have at most four prime coordinates), checks
the exact-rank-prime Mobius formula on every nonempty support, and verifies that
every canonical and weighted rank-pure product belongs to the independently
constructed minimal-generator set. It also confirms that the rank-pure sector
is strict at `n=91`, where `169` is a ladder generator. The exact
Hearne--Wagner and connected-cover counts are
cross-checked against direct cover enumeration for `1 <= k <= 4`. Exact counts
through `k=80` also check convergence to the parity-dependent theta
normalization, the connected-cover ratio, and the central discrete local
limit. On every `3 <= n <= 210` layer the report also checks the pointwise
rank-window deaggregation inequalities and the refined private-coordinate
upper bound; on every squarefree layer it checks the exact-rank partition and
the BLMS pigeonhole inequality. It also reconstructs the fibotomic integer on
every rank in this range, verifies its exact-rank radical divisibility and the
rank-congruence spacing used in the entropy bound, and checks `a(10p) >= 2`
for the five eligible prime values with `10p <= 210`. In addition, it extracts
the positive-coordinate rank hypergraph of every minimal generator, verifies
the total and connected support spectra on all 208 layers, and checks that the
top-support slice has the product cardinality of its singleton diagonal atomic
families. It confirms that the only empty exact-rank prime fibers on
`3 <= d <= 210` are `d=6,12`, verifies
`7 <- 13 <- 233 <- 139801`, and checks `7 -> 8 -> 6 -> 12` by direct modular
rank computation. These finite checks support, but do not prove, the infinite
inverse-ray theorem; infinitude uses the classical exact-rank existence
theorem. These finite checks do not resolve the
asymptotic behavior of `R(n)`, establish (H2) or (BW), or compare the total
connected and disconnected arithmetic sectors without those hypotheses.

The squarefree-slice verifier independently enumerates all irredundant covers
for `1 <= k <= 4`, checks 36 exact full-support equality profiles for the
sharp weighted-cover lower bound, and compares direct rank-pure products with
the squarefree elements of the independently constructed `M_n` for every
squarefree `3 <= n <= 210`. The stored run contains 127 set equalities and 427
squarefree minimal generators. These checks do not show that the abstract
fixed-total extremizer is realizable by Fibonacci exact-rank primes.

\section{Rank-pure covers and the canonical squarefree slice}
\label{sec:rank-pure}

For a finite set $V$, write $\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ for the set of
irredundant covers of $V$ by distinct nonempty subsets: a family
$\mathcal C$ belongs to $\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ when
$\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal C}S=V$ and every $S\in\mathcal C$ contains an element
outside $\bigcup_{T\in\mathcal C\setminus\{S\}}T$.

\begin{theorem}[Rank-pure universality]\label{thm:rank-pure-universality}
Let $n\ge3$, put $V=\mathcal P(n)$, and, for every nonempty $S\subseteq V$,
define
\[
 n_S:=\prod_{\ell\in S}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)},\qquad
 a_n(S):=\#\Pi_\alpha(n_S),
\]
and
\[
 \mathcal E_n:=\{S\subseteq V:S\ne\varnothing,\ n_S\in\{2,6,12\}\}.
\]
Then $\#\mathcal E_n\le2$, and every member of $\mathcal E_n$ has cardinality
at most two. Define the rank-pure prime sector
\[
 \mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}:=
 \left\{
 \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}p_S:
 \begin{array}{l}
  \mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V),\quad
  \mathcal C\cap\mathcal E_n=\varnothing,\\
  p_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)\quad(S\in\mathcal C)
 \end{array}
 \right\}.
\]
Then $\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}\subseteq\mathcal M_n$, the displayed representation is
unique, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector}
 \#\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}=
 \sum_{\substack{\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)\\
                   \mathcal C\cap\mathcal E_n=\varnothing}}
 \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}a_n(S),
 \qquad
 a_n(S)=\sum_{d\mid n_S}\mu(n_S/d)\,\omega(F_d).
\end{equation}
Moreover, the support hypergraph of the product attached to $\mathcal C$ has
edge set exactly $\mathcal C$. In particular, connected minimal covers map to
$\mathcal M_n^{\rm conn}$, with their complete hypergraph structure preserved.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The map $S\mapsto n_S$ is injective. If $n_S=2$, then
$S=\{2\}$ and $\nu_2(n)=1$. If $n_S=6$, then
$S=\{2,3\}$ and $\nu_2(n)=\nu_3(n)=1$; if $n_S=12$, then
$S=\{2,3\}$, $\nu_2(n)=2$, and $\nu_3(n)=1$. Thus the ranks $6$ and $12$
cannot both occur, while rank $2$ can coexist only with rank $6$. This proves
the assertions about $\mathcal E_n$.

Fix $\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ avoiding $\mathcal E_n$. For
every $S\in\mathcal C$, the integer $n_S$ is at least $3$ and is not $6$ or
$12$. Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors} therefore gives a prime
$p_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)$. Distinct supports have distinct ranks, so the primes
$p_S$ are distinct. Since $p_S$ is a prime atom and $d^-(p_S)=1$,
\[
 E_n(p_S)=T_n(p_S)=S.
\]
Consequently the coverage and private-coordinate conditions defining
$\operatorname{MinCov}(V)$ are exactly the witness-cover conditions for
$\{p_S:S\in\mathcal C\}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-classification} places
$\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}p_S$ in $\mathcal M_n$.

Unique factorization recovers every selected prime from the product; its rank
$\alpha(p_S)=n_S$ then recovers $S$. Hence both the cover and all prime choices
are recovered, proving injectivity and the weighted count in
\eqref{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector}. The formula for $a_n(S)$ is
Lemma~\ref{lem:primitive-prime-mobius-count}. Finally,
\[
 \operatorname{supp}_n(\alpha(p_S))=
 \operatorname{supp}_n(n_S)=S,
\]
so Definition~\ref{def:support-hypergraph} gives precisely the edge set
$\mathcal C$ and therefore preserves connected components.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Canonical minimal-transversal model]
\label{cor:rank-pure-minimal-transversals}
Retain the notation of Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality}. Define a
hypergraph $\mathcal H_n^{\rm rp}$ with vertex set
\[
 U_n:=\{(S,p):\varnothing\ne S\subseteq V,\ S\notin\mathcal E_n,
                    \ p\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)\}
\]
and, for each $\ell\in V$, the coordinate edge
\[
 X_\ell:=\{(S,p)\in U_n:\ell\in S\}.
\]
Multiplication induces a bijection
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-pure-minimal-transversal-bijection}
 \operatorname{MinTr}(\mathcal H_n^{\rm rp})
 \longrightarrow \mathcal R_n^{\rm rp},
 \qquad Q\longmapsto\prod_{(S,p)\in Q}p,
\end{equation}
where $\operatorname{MinTr}$ denotes the inclusion-minimal transversals.
Hence the weighted cover polynomial
\eqref{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector} is exactly a minimal-transversal count of
a canonically defined hypergraph. By the standard split-incidence
correspondence \cite[Proposition~30]{KanteLimouzyMaryNourine2014DominatingSets},
it also counts the minimal connected dominating sets of the associated split
graph.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
A set $Q\subseteq U_n$ is a transversal precisely when its support labels
cover $V$. If a minimal transversal contained $(S,p)$ and $(S,q)$ with
$p\ne q$, removing either vertex would preserve every coordinate edge hit by
it, a contradiction. Thus its support labels are distinct. Minimality also
says that every $(S,p)\in Q$ has a coordinate $\ell\in S$ belonging to no
other selected support. The support labels therefore form an irredundant
cover. Conversely, selected prime choices over an irredundant cover form a
minimal transversal, since every selected vertex has such a private
coordinate edge. Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} and unique
factorization now prove that multiplication is the asserted bijection.
\end{proof}

The rank-pure sector has a canonical description on squarefree indices.  In
particular, it is not then an auxiliary class defined only through the cover
construction.

\begin{corollary}[Canonical squarefree exact-fiber slice]
\label{cor:canonical-squarefree-slice}
Let $n\ge3$ be squarefree.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:canonical-squarefree-slice}
 \mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}
 =\mathcal M_n\cap\{q:\mu^2(q)=1\}
 =\min_{\mid}\{q:\alpha(q)=n,\ \mu^2(q)=1\}.
\end{equation}
Thus the weighted sum in
\eqref{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector} counts exactly the divisibility-minimal
squarefree elements of the standard exact fiber $\alpha^{-1}(n)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Proposition~\ref{prop:rank-basics} gives
$B_n=\{q:\alpha(q)=n\}=\alpha^{-1}(n)$.  If a squarefree member of $B_n$
has a proper divisor in $B_n$, that divisor is again squarefree.  Hence
minimality in the squarefree part of the exact fiber is equivalent to
membership in $\mathcal M_n$.

Every element of $\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}$ is a product of distinct primes and
belongs to $\mathcal M_n$, which proves one inclusion in the first equality.
Conversely, let $m\in\mathcal M_n$ be squarefree.  Its unique atomic
factorization from Theorem~\ref{thm:witness-classification} consists only of

 succeeded in 241ms:
\[
 T_{91}(169)=\{7,13\},\qquad E_{91}(169)=\{13\}.
\]
The singleton cover $\{169\}$ is irredundant, and its product is $169$, so
$169\in\mathcal M_{91}$. It is a squareful ladder atom and therefore lies
outside the prime-only rank-pure sector:
\[
 169\in\mathcal M_{91}\setminus\mathcal R_{91}^{\rm rp}.
\]
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Odd-layer cover universality]
\label{cor:odd-rank-pure-universality}
Let $n\ge3$ be odd and put $k=\omega(n)$. Then $\mathcal E_n=\varnothing$.
After fixing one prime $\pi_S\in\Pi_\alpha(n_S)$ for every nonempty
$S\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, the map
\[
 \mathcal C\longmapsto\prod_{S\in\mathcal C}\pi_S
\]
embeds $\operatorname{MinCov}(\mathcal P(n))$ into $\mathcal M_n$ and
preserves the support hypergraph. Hence, if $C_k$ denotes the number of minimal
covers of a labelled $k$-set, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-minimal-cover-bound}
 \#\mathcal M_n\ge C_k,
 \qquad
 \widthop(B_n)\ge C_k.
\end{equation}
Through Royle's correspondence
\cite{Royle2000SetCoversSplitGraphs}, every isomorphism type of split graph on
$k$ vertices consequently has a rank-pure Fibonacci realization on the prime
coordinates of $n$. This last sentence concerns unlabelled isomorphism types
only and is not used to enumerate the labelled covers below.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Every nonempty $n_S$ is odd and at least $3$, so no support is exceptional.
Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality} gives the embedding and support
preservation. Minimal generators form an antichain, which proves the width
bound. Royle's theorem is a correspondence between isomorphism types of split
graphs and minimal covers; choosing a labelling of a representative cover and
then applying the embedding gives the final assertion.
\end{proof}

We next fix the classical enumeration input. Let $C_{k,s}$ count the minimal
covers of a labelled $k$-set having
$s$ members, put $C_k=\sum_s C_{k,s}$, and put
\[
 b_k:=\sum_{s=0}^k\binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)}.
\]
Hearne and Wagner \cite{HearneWagner1973MinimalCovers} proved
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hearne-wagner}
 C_{k,s}=\sum_{j=s}^k\binom{k}{j}
 \genfrac{\{}{\}}{0pt}{}{j}{s}
 (2^s-s-1)^{k-j}.
\end{equation}
The prime coordinates of $n$ are specific primes, so the arithmetic embedding
uses these labelled covers: there is no quotient by $S_k$ and no division by
$k!$. Royle's correspondence above concerns isomorphism classes and supplies
only the realization statement. The labelled central sum $b_k$ is the object
in the split-graph enumeration of
Bender--Richmond--Wormald \cite{BenderRichmondWormald1985ChordalSplit}, in the
form recorded by Troyka \cite[Section~3]{Troyka2019SplitGraphs}. The following
direct comparison closes the conversion from the Hearne--Wagner labelled
formula to that labelled central sum.

\begin{lemma}[Labelled minimal-cover comparison]
\label{lem:labelled-minimal-cover-comparison}
As $k\to\infty$,
\[
 \frac{C_k}{b_k}\longrightarrow1.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix an order on the labelled ground set. In a minimal cover with $s$ members,
select the least private element of each member. The selected elements form an
$s$-set $P$; no selected element can occur in another member, and each member
is recovered from its selected element and a subset of the remaining
$k-s$ elements. This canonical encoding is injective, so
\[
 C_{k,s}\le\binom{k}{s}2^{s(k-s)}.
\]
The $j=s$ term of \eqref{eq:hearne-wagner} gives the matching lower bound
\[
 C_{k,s}\ge\binom{k}{s}(2^s-s-1)^{k-s}.
\]
Uniformly for $k/3\le s\le2k/3$,
\[
 \left(1-\frac{s+1}{2^s}\right)^{k-s}=1-o(1).
\]
The contribution to $b_k$ from the complementary values of $s$ is at most
\[
 2^k2^{2k^2/9}=o\bigl(2^{\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor}\bigr)=o(b_k).
\]
Summing the two bounds over the central range therefore gives
$(1-o(1))b_k\le C_k\le b_k$, which proves the claim.
\end{proof}

Consequently, with
\[
 \vartheta_0:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb Z}2^{-r^2},\qquad
 \vartheta_1:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb Z}2^{-(r+1/2)^2},\qquad
 \varepsilon\equiv k\pmod2,
\]
the published labelled asymptotic for $b_k$ and
Lemma~\ref{lem:labelled-minimal-cover-comparison} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:minimal-cover-theta}
 C_k\sim b_k\sim
 \vartheta_\varepsilon\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}2^{k^2/4}.
\end{equation}
In particular, $b_{k-1}/b_k=O(2^{-k/2})$.

\begin{corollary}[Weighted-cover obstruction on odd layers]
\label{cor:odd-weighted-cover-obstruction}
Let $n\ge3$ be odd, put $V=\mathcal P(n)$ and $k=\omega(n)$, and retain the
weights $a_n(S)=\#\Pi_\alpha(n_S)$ from
Theorem~\ref{thm:rank-pure-universality}. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-weighted-rank-pure-sector}
 \#\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}
 =\sum_{\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)}
       \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}a_n(S)
 \le\#\mathcal M_n.
\end{equation}
Every weight in this sum is positive. Consequently, with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-cover-excess}
 \mathcal W(n):=\frac1{C_k}
 \sum_{\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)}
       \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}a_n(S)\ge1,
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:odd-rank-pure-log-decomposition}
 \log\#\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}
 =\frac{\log2}{4}k^2+\log\mathcal W(n)+O(k).
\end{equation}
Therefore an almost-all asymptotic
\[
 \log\#\mathcal M_n\sim\frac{\log2}{4}(\log\log n)^2
\]
would necessarily imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-cover-dispersion-interface}
 \log\mathcal W(n)=o\bigl((\log\log n)^2\bigr)
\end{equation}
outside an $o(x)$ exceptional set of odd $n\le x$.
The converse is not asserted: ladder atoms and other non-rank-pure witness
covers still require an upper bound.  This distinction occurs already at the
odd squarefree index $91$, where $\alpha(13)=7$ and
$\alpha(13^2)=91$, so $169\in\mathcal M_{91}\setminus\mathcal R_{91}^{\rm rp}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For odd $n$, every $n_S$ is odd and at least $3$, so
$\mathcal E_n=\varnothing$ and the exact-rank existence theorem gives
$a_n(S)\ge1$.  The weighted identity and inclusion are
\eqref{eq:rank-pure-weighted-sector}.  The asymptotic
\eqref{eq:minimal-cover-theta} gives
$\log C_k=(\log2)k^2/4+O(k)$ and proves
\eqref{eq:odd-rank-pure-log-decomposition}.

If the displayed almost-all formula for $\mathcal M_n$ held, then it would
hold outside an $o(x)$ subset of the odd integers.  Intersecting with the
Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order set and using
$\#\mathcal R_n^{\rm rp}\le\#\mathcal M_n$ forces
\eqref{eq:weighted-cover-dispersion-interface}.  Finally, the classical
lifting formula gives $\alpha(13^2)=13\alpha(13)=91$ and
$\alpha(13)=7<91$.  Thus $13^2$ is an atom of rank $91$ and the singleton
witness cover $\{13^2\}$ gives the stated strict inclusion.
\end{proof}

The following sharp extremal calculation separates total primitive-prime
mass from the dispersion information retained by the cover polynomial.

\begin{proposition}[Sharp cumulative-data obstruction]
\label{prop:sharp-cumulative-data-obstruction}
Let $V$ be a labelled $k$-set, $k\ge1$, and assign a real weight
$w_S\ge1$ to every nonempty $S\subseteq V$.  Put
\[
 Z_V(w):=\sum_{\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)}
              \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}w_S,
 \qquad A:=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq V}w_S.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sharp-total-mass-cover-bound}
 Z_V(w)\ge C_k+A-(2^k-1).
\end{equation}
This is best possible for every $A\ge2^k-1$: equality is attained by
\[
 w_V=A-(2^k-2),\qquad w_S=1\quad(S\ne V).
\]
If $k\ge3$, this is the only equality profile.

Consequently, if $n\ge3$ is odd and squarefree and $k=\omega(n)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-cumulative-rank-pure-bound}
 \#\{m\in\mathcal M_n:\mu^2(m)=1\}
 \ge C_k+\omega(F_n)-(2^k-1),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-cumulative-weighted-excess}
 \mathcal W(n)\ge
 1+\frac{\omega(F_n)-(2^k-1)}{C_k}.
\end{equation}
Among all positive weight profiles with the same total
$\sum_{d\mid n,\ d>1}a(d)=\omega(F_n)$, this conclusion is exact.  The
extremizing profile is an abstract information-theoretic model; it is not
asserted to be realized by Fibonacci exact-rank primes.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $w_S=1+x_S$ with $x_S\ge0$.  For every minimal cover $\mathcal C$,
\[
 \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}(1+x_S)
 \ge1+\sum_{S\in\mathcal C}x_S.
\]
Every nonempty $S\subseteq V$ occurs in a minimal cover: use $\{V\}$ when
$S=V$, and otherwise use
$\{S\}\cup\{\{v\}:v\in V\setminus S\}$.  Summing the preceding inequality
over all minimal covers therefore gives
\[
 Z_V(w)\ge C_k+\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq V}x_S
 =C_k+A-(2^k-1).
\]

\section{Support entropy and the unresolved arithmetic multiplicity}
\label{sec:growth}
%% ====================================================================

Write $\Omega(N)=\sum_p\nu_p(N)$ for the number of prime factors of $N$
counted with multiplicity. Set $R(2)=1$, consistently with
$\mathcal M_2=\varnothing$. For $n\ge3$ define the maximal atomic rank-window
multiplicity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rank-window-multiplicity}
 R(n):=\max\!\left\{1,
 \max_{\varnothing\ne I\subseteq J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}
       \#\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)\right\}.
\end{equation}
By the prime--ladder alternative, the nonsingleton families counted here are the
exact-rank windows $P_J(n)$, whereas every ladder family contributes at most
one atom. Thus $R(n)$ records the maximal atomic-family multiplicity retained
by the counting bounds below. It is an exact parameter for those bounds, but
it is not asserted to be an information-theoretically complete invariant of
the arithmetic outside the support combinatorics.

We first remove the contribution caused solely by collecting several exact
rank classes in one support window. For $d\mid n$, write
\[
 T_n(d):=\{\ell\in\mathcal P(n):
 \nu_\ell(d)=\nu_\ell(n)\}.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Mean rank-window deaggregation]
\label{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation}
Put
\[
 a(d):=\#\Pi_\alpha(d),\qquad
 \mathcal D^+(n):=\{d\mid n:T_n(d)\ne\varnothing\},
\]
and
\[
 A^*(n):=\max\left\{1,\max_{d\in\mathcal D^+(n)}a(d)\right\}.
\]
Then, as $x\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mean-window-deaggregation}
 \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
 =\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n)
 +O(x\log\log x).
\end{equation}
Consequently the mean sparse-window hypothesis \textup{(H2)} is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}
 \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)
 \log\max\left\{1,
 \max_{\substack{d\mid n\\T_n(d)\ne\varnothing}}
 \#\Pi_\alpha(d)\right\}
 =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For nonempty $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, let
\[
 W_J(n):=\{d\mid n:T_n(d)=J\}.
\]
If $n=\prod_\ell\ell^{e_\ell}$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:support-window-size}
 \#W_J(n)=\prod_{\ell\notin J}e_\ell.
\end{equation}
The prime atoms in the family with essential and full supports $(J,J)$ form
the finite disjoint union
\[
 \coprod_{d\in W_J(n)}\Pi_\alpha(d),
\]
because a prime has a unique rank of apparition. By the prime--ladder
alternative and the unique ladder-slot formula in
Lemma~\ref{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:atomic-family-ladder-slots}, every remaining effective
atomic family is a ladder family with singleton essential support and at most
one atom. Therefore, with
\[
 P(n):=\max_{\varnothing\ne J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)}
       \sum_{d\in W_J(n)}a(d),
\]
one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:prime-window-versus-R}
 P(n)\le R(n)\le P(n)+1.
\end{equation}
Every $d\in\mathcal D^+(n)$ belongs to one of the windows above. Hence
\[
 A^*(n)\le R(n)
 \le1+A^*(n)\max_J\#W_J(n)
 \le1+A^*(n)\prod_{\ell^{e_\ell}\parallel n}e_\ell.
\]
Writing
\[
 h(n):=\sum_{\ell^{e_\ell}\parallel n}\log e_\ell,
\]
and using $A^*(n)\ge1$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation}
 0\le\log R(n)-\log A^*(n)\le\log2+h(n).
\end{equation}
For each exponent $e\ge2$, $\log e\le\sum_{2\le j\le e}\log j$, so
\[
 h(n)\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\mid n\\j\ge2}}\log j.
\]
The classical mean estimate \eqref{eq:mean-omega-input}, used uniformly with
$\log\log(3y)$ in place of $\log\log y$ for small $y$, yields
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)h(n)
 &\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\le x\\j\ge2}}\log j
       \sum_{m\le x/p^j}\omega(p^jm)\\
 &\le\sum_{\substack{p^j\le x\\j\ge2}}\log j
       \sum_{m\le x/p^j}(1+\omega(m))\\
 &\ll x\log\log x\sum_p\sum_{j\ge2}\frac{\log j}{p^j}
 \ll x\log\log x.
\end{align*}
The double series converges absolutely. Summing
\eqref{eq:pointwise-window-deaggregation} and using
$\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\ll x\log\log x$ proves
\eqref{eq:mean-window-deaggregation}; the stated equivalence follows because
$x\log\log x=o(x(\log\log x)^2)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Single-rank sufficient and necessary interfaces]
\label{cor:single-rank-mean-interfaces}
A sufficient condition for \textup{(H2)} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:single-rank-sufficient-condition}
 \sum_{d\le x}
 \frac{(\omega(d)+\log\log x)\log\max\{1,a(d)\}}{d}
 =o\bigl((\log\log x)^2\bigr).
\end{equation}
Conversely, \textup{(H2)} implies the diagonal necessary condition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:single-rank-necessary-condition}
 \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log\max\{1,a(n)\}
 =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since
\[
 \log A^*(n)\le\sum_{d\mid n}\log\max\{1,a(d)\},
\]
the substitution $n=dm$ and $\omega(dm)\le\omega(d)+\omega(m)$ gives
\[
 \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)\log A^*(n)
 \ll x\sum_{d\le x}
 \frac{(\omega(d)+\log\log x)\log\max\{1,a(d)\}}{d}.
\]
Thus \eqref{eq:single-rank-sufficient-condition} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:mean-rank-window-deaggregation} imply \textup{(H2)}. For the
necessary condition, $n\in\mathcal D^+(n)$, and hence
$R(n)\ge A^*(n)\ge\max\{1,a(n)\}$; summation proves the claim.
\end{proof}

Corollary~\ref{cor:atomic-classification}, $\theta$ is a ladder atom.

For nonempty $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$, define the prime window
\[
P_J(n):=\coprod_{\substack{d\mid n\\T_n(d)=J}}\Pi_\alpha(d),
\]
where $T_n(d)=\{\ell\in\mathcal P(n):

\begin{proposition}[Atomic families and unique ladder slots]
\label{prop:atomic-family-ladder-slots}
Let $n\ge3$ and let $\varnothing\ne I\subseteq J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$.
Then every atom in $\mathcal A_{I,J}(n)$ is either in the prime window
$P_J(n)$, which occurs only when $I=J$, or in a ladder family with
$I=\{p\}$ for a prime coordinate $p\in J$.  Each such ladder family contains
at most one atom.

More precisely, put $a=\nu_p(n)$.  Its only possible ladder atom is
\[
\lambda_p(n)=
\begin{cases}
4, & p=2,\ a=1,\\
2^{a+2}, & p=2,\ a\ge2,\\
5^a, & p=5,\ a\ge2,\\
p^{h_p+a}, & p\notin\{2,5\},
\end{cases}
\]
with no candidate for $p=5$, $a=1$.  The candidate belongs to
$\mathcal A_{\{p\},J}(n)$ exactly when
$d(\lambda_p(n))\mid n$ and $T_n(\lambda_p(n))=J$.
Consequently every atomic family is finite, every nonsingleton atomic family
is a finite disjoint union of exact-rank prime classes, and every labelled
ladder slot has at most one candidate.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}

$\Pi_\alpha(d(q))$ in $P_{T_n(q)}(n)$. Conversely every prime in $P_J(n)$
has $(E_n,T_n)=(J,J)$.

Suppose $\theta=q^e$ is a ladder atom. The same dichotomy gives

$2^e$ of rank $3\cdot2^{e-2}$ for $e\ge4$. For $p=5$, the rank is $5^e$,
and $e=1$ is a prime atom. For $p\notin\{2,5\}$, one has

\begin{theorem}[Multiplicity-free support entropy with arithmetic remainder]
\label{thm:minimal-generator-growth}
For every integer $n\ge3$, put $k=\omega(n)$ and
$L(n)=\log\#\mathcal M_n$. Then $\mathcal M_n$ is nonempty. If $k\ge3$ and
$r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}
 (2^r-1)^{k-r}
 \le \#\mathcal M_n
 \le \sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}.
\end{equation}
In particular, uniformly along every sequence for which $\omega(n)\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}
 \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2-O(1)
 \le L(n)
 \le \frac{\log2}{4}\,\omega(n)^2
      +\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n)).
\end{equation}
No exact quadratic coefficient for the full quantity $L(n)$ is claimed.
The coefficient $\log2/4$ is exact only for the multiplicity-free
minimal-cover contribution: the lower construction embeds the unweighted
cover count, and the private-coordinate encoding gives the same quadratic
support term in the upper bound. The display is not an asymptotic formula for
$L(n)$ unless $R(n)=\exp(o(\omega(n)))$; no such unconditional estimate for
$R(n)$ is proved here. In particular, the displayed coefficient is not
claimed to be optimal among all deductions from fibotomic or exact-rank
arithmetic information.

For comparison, the crude support-count estimate gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}
 \#\mathcal M_n
 \le \sum_{r=0}^{k}\binom{\Omega(F_n)}{r}
 \le n^k.
\end{equation}
Combining only the last inequality with the classical maximal order of
$\omega(n)$ gives the crude corollary
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:crude-maximal-order}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{L(n)\log\log n}{(\log n)^2}\le1.
\end{equation}
This envelope has scale $(\log n)^2/\log\log n$, a factor $\log\log n$
above the witness-cover entropy scale; it is not a consequence of
\eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.
At the coarser divisor-lattice scale, the antichain property and Wigert's
theorem give the Sperner-type envelope
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}
 L(n)\le\log\tau(F_n)
 \le (\log2\,\log\varphi+o(1))\frac{n}{\log n},
 \qquad \varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt5}{2};
\end{equation}
in fact \eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} is stronger and implies
$L(n)=o(n/\log n)$.

Moreover, as $x\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}
 \left(\frac{\log2}{4}+o(1)\right)x(\log\log x)^2
 \le \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 \le (1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
\end{equation}

\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
First, $F_n\in B_n$ for every $n\ge3$.  Indeed,
$F_n\mid F_d$ implies $F_n\le F_d$, and strict increase of the Fibonacci
sequence from index $2$ onward forces $d\ge n$; hence $\alpha(F_n)=n$.
Because $B_n$ is a nonempty finite poset, it has a minimal element, so
$\mathcal M_n\ne\varnothing$ and $L(n)$ is defined.

We first prove the explicit finite private-cover bound in
\eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}. Fix $k\ge3$, and give
$\mathcal P(n)$ an arbitrary order. Put
\[
 X=\mathcal P(n)\cap\{2,3\},\qquad r=\lfloor k/2\rfloor.
\]
Since $k-\#X\ge k-2\ge r$, choose an $r$-element set
$P\subseteq\mathcal P(n)\setminus X$, and put
$Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. For every map
\[
 H:Q\longrightarrow 2^P\setminus\{\varnothing\}
\]
and every $i\in P$, define
\[
 T_i=\{i\}\cup\{q\in Q:i\in H(q)\}.
\]
The index $n_{T_i}=\prod_{\ell\in T_i}\ell^{\nu_\ell(n)}$ has a prime
divisor outside $\{2,3\}$, because $i\in P$. Hence $n_{T_i}\ge5$ and
$n_{T_i}\notin\{6,12\}$. Theorem~\ref{thm:primitive-divisors} supplies a
prime $\pi_{T_i}$ with $\alpha(\pi_{T_i})=n_{T_i}$; fix one such prime for
each support that occurs.

The supports $T_i$ cover $\mathcal P(n)$ because $H(q)$ is nonempty for every
$q\in Q$. Moreover, $i$ belongs to $T_i$ and to no $T_j$ with $j\ne i$.
Each $\pi_{T_i}$ is a prime atom, so
$E_n(\pi_{T_i})=T_n(\pi_{T_i})=T_i$. Thus
$\{\pi_{T_i}:i\in P\}$ is an $n$-witness cover. Distinct supports have
distinct ranks and therefore distinct underlying primes. If two maps $H$
differ, then their support families differ, since the edge containing the
private coordinate $i$ identifies $T_i$; unique factorization then gives
different minimal generators. There are
$(2^r-1)^{\#Q}=(2^r-1)^{k-r}$ maps $H$, proving the lower bound in
\eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.

For the upper bound, let $\Theta$ be an $n$-witness cover with $s$ atoms. For
each $\theta\in\Theta$, choose the least coordinate
\[
 x_\theta\in E_n(\theta)\setminus
 \bigcup_{\eta\in\Theta\setminus\{\theta\}}T_n(\eta).
\]
These private coordinates are distinct; write
$P=\{x_\theta:\theta\in\Theta\}$ and
$Q=\mathcal P(n)\setminus P$. If $x_\theta=x$, then
\[
 T_n(\theta)=\{x\}\cup U_\theta,\qquad U_\theta\subseteq Q,
\]
because every other member of $P$ is private to another atom. By the
prime--ladder alternative in Lemma~\ref{thm:primitive-ladder-dichotomy}, either
$E_n(\theta)=T_n(\theta)$ or $E_n(\theta)=\{x\}$. Hence there are at most two
support-pair alternatives, and the definition
\eqref{eq:rank-window-multiplicity} allows at most $R(n)$ atoms for each.
Thus, once $P$ is fixed, the atom indexed by each
$x\in P$ has at most $2R(n)2^{k-s}$ choices. Ignoring coverage and
coprimeness only enlarges the count, so summing over the
$\binom{k}{s}$ possible sets $P$ and over $1\le s\le k$ proves the upper
bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-finite-bounds}.

Now $r(k-r)=\lfloor k^2/4\rfloor$ and
$(k-r)\log(1-2^{-r})=O(1)$. Hence the logarithm of the lower bound is
$(\log2)k^2/4+O(1)$. For the upper bound, use
$s(k-s)\le k^2/4$, $s\le k$, and
$\sum_s\binom{k}{s}\le2^k$ to obtain
\[
 \log\sum_{s=1}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(2R(n))^s2^{s(k-s)}
 \le\frac{\log2}{4}k^2+k\log R(n)+O(k).
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}.

Let
\[
 \mathcal Q_n=\{p^e:p\mid F_n,\ 1\le e\le\nu_p(F_n)\}.
\]
This set has cardinality $\Omega(F_n)$.  By unique factorization, the map
\[
 m\longmapsto\{p^{\nu_p(m)}:p\mid m\}
\]
is injective from $\mathcal M_n$ into the subsets of $\mathcal Q_n$.
Corollary~\ref{cor:support-bound} says that every image has at most
$k=\omega(n)$ members.  This proves the first inequality in
\eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.

Since every prime factor of $F_n$ is at least $2$,
$2^{\Omega(F_n)}\le F_n$.  Also $F_n<2^{n-1}$ for $n\ge3$, by the elementary
induction $F_{j+1}=F_j+F_{j-1}<2^j$.  Hence
$\Omega(F_n)\le n-2$.  A subset of an $N$-element set having at most $k$
members can be encoded injectively by its increasing list padded to length
$k$ with a new symbol; therefore
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^k\binom Nr\le(N+1)^k.
\]
Taking $N=\Omega(F_n)$ gives the second inequality in
\eqref{eq:finite-minimal-generator-bound}.

The classical maximal-order formula for $\omega(n)$
\cite[Theorem~22.11]{HardyWright2008Introduction}
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{\omega(n)\log\log n}{\log n}=1
\]
now combines with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$ to prove
\eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order}. This argument uses only the crude estimate
$\#\mathcal M_n\le n^{\omega(n)}$, not the witness-cover entropy bound. On
the other hand,
$\mathcal M_n$ is an antichain in the divisor lattice of $F_n$, so
\[
 \#\mathcal M_n\le\widthop(\operatorname{Div}(F_n))\le\tau(F_n).
\]
Wigert's maximal-order theorem \cite{Wigert1907Divisors} states that
\[
 \log\tau(N)\le(\log2+o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}.
\]
Binet's formula gives $\log F_n=n\log\varphi+O(1)$ and
$\log\log F_n=\log n+O(1)$, yielding
\eqref{eq:sperner-wigert-envelope}.  Finally,
$(\log n)^2/\log\log n=o(n/\log n)$, so
\eqref{eq:crude-maximal-order} gives the stated strict improvement.

For the mean upper bound, use the classical mean-value estimate
\cite{HardyRamanujan1917NormalPrimeFactors}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:mean-omega-input}
 \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=x\log\log x+O(x),
\end{equation}
together with $L(n)\le\omega(n)\log n$.  Uniformly for $n\le x$,
$\log n\le\log x$, and hence
\[
 \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 \le\log x\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)
 =(1+o(1))x\log x\,\log\log x.
\]

Tur\'an's second-moment form of the Hardy--Ramanujan theorem
\cite{Turan1934HardyRamanujan} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:omega-second-moment}
 \sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)^2=(1+o(1))x(\log\log x)^2.
\end{equation}
After enlarging the absolute constant in the lower half of
\eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, it holds as
$L(n)\ge(\log2)\omega(n)^2/4-O(1)$ also for $\omega(n)<3$.
Summation and \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment} prove the lower half of
\eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth} and complete the proof.
\end{proof}


\begin{definition}[Sufficient maximum-window hypothesis H1]
\label{hyp:normal-window}
For every $\varepsilon>0$,
\[
 \#\{n\le x:\log R(n)>\varepsilon\omega(n)\}=o(x).
\tag{H1}
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Sufficient maximum-window hypothesis H2]
\label{hyp:mean-window}
One has
\[
 \sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)
 =o\bigl(x(\log\log x)^2\bigr).
\tag{H2}
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Conditional obstruction from Fibonacci prime divisors]
\label{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window}
Assume Conjecture~5.1 of Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek
\cite{BugeaudLucaMignotteSiksek2005FewPrimeDivisors}, namely that
$\omega(F_n)\gg\log n$ for composite $n$. Then, for every fixed
$\varepsilon$ with $0<\varepsilon<1-\log2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:blms-normal-window-obstruction}
 \liminf_{x\to\infty}\frac1x
 \#\{n\le x:\log R(n)>\varepsilon\omega(n)\}
 \ge\frac6{\pi^2},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}
 \liminf_{x\to\infty}
 \frac{\sum_{3\le n\le x}\omega(n)\log R(n)}
 {x(\log\log x)^2}
 \ge\frac6{\pi^2}(1-\log2)>0.
\end{equation}
In particular, under that published conjecture, both sufficient
maximum-window hypotheses \textup{(H1)} and \textup{(H2)} are false.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $n$ be squarefree and put $k=\omega(n)$. The exact-rank partition of the
prime divisors of $F_n$ gives
\[
 \omega(F_n)=\sum_{d\mid n}a(d).
\]
For squarefree $n$, every nonempty support $J\subseteq\mathcal P(n)$ has the
single rank $d=\prod_{\ell\in J}\ell$ in its window. Since $a(1)=0$, the
pigeonhole principle and \eqref{eq:prime-window-versus-R} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-blms-pigeonhole}
 R(n)\ge\max_{d\mid n}a(d)
 \ge\frac{\omega(F_n)}{2^k-1}.
\end{equation}
Under the stated conjecture, for every sufficiently large squarefree composite
$n$,
\[
 \log R(n)\ge\log\log n-k\log2+O(1).
\]
The Hardy--Ramanujan theorem has an exceptional set of $o(x)$ integers, so on
all but $o(x)$ squarefree integers one has
$k=(1+o(1))\log\log n$. Consequently
\[
 \omega(n)\log R(n)
 \ge(1-\log2+o(1))(\log\log n)^2
\]
on this set. Squarefree integers have density $6/\pi^2$, and squarefree prime
indices have density zero. Moreover,
\[
 \frac{\log R(n)}{\omega(n)}\ge1-\log2+o(1)
\]
on the same set. This proves
\eqref{eq:blms-normal-window-obstruction}; summation over squarefree composite
indices proves \eqref{eq:blms-mean-window-obstruction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Purely conditional consequences of H1 and H2]
\label{cor:conditional-orders}
Assuming hypothesis \textup{(H1)} in Definition~\ref{hyp:normal-window},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-normal-order}
 L(n)\sim\frac{\log2}{4}(\log\log n)^2
 \quad\text{for almost all }n.
\end{equation}
Assuming hypothesis \textup{(H2)} in Definition~\ref{hyp:mean-window},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-mean-order}
 \sum_{3\le n\le x}L(n)
 \sim\frac{\log2}{4}x(\log\log x)^2.
\end{equation}
These are implications only: neither conclusion is unconditional, and no
positive arithmetic evidence for either sufficient hypothesis is presently
known. Proposition~\ref{prop:blms-obstructs-mean-window} shows that the
published Bugeaud--Luca--Mignotte--Siksek conjecture predicts that both
hypotheses fail.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For the first assertion, intersect the density-one set in
Definition~\ref{hyp:normal-window} with the Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order set.
Then the error $\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n))$ in
\eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} is $o(\omega(n)^2)$. For the second,
sum the upper bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, use
Definition~\ref{hyp:mean-window}, \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment}, and
$\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=O(x\log\log x)$, and compare with the unconditional
lower mean bound \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}.
\end{proof}

Supplementary Corollary~\ref{supp-cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} determine the contribution of the
multiplicity-free minimal-cover model used here: $\log2/4$ is its exact
quadratic coefficient, the fixed-choice rank-pure sector supplies its
theta-refined lower terms, and the private-coordinate upper bound retains the
term $\omega(n)\log R(n)$. Proposition~

\section{Preliminaries}\label{sec:prelim}

\subsection{Rank of apparition and Fibonacci divisibility}\label{subsec:fib-div}

\begin{lemma}[Finite rank of apparition]\label{lem:finite-rank}
For every integer $q\ge1$, the rank of apparition $\alpha(q)$ is finite.  More
precisely, $\alpha(1)=1$, and for every $q\ge2$ there is a positive integer
$r$ such that $q\mid F_r$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The case $q=1$ is the stated convention.  Let $q\ge2$.  Work modulo $q$ with
the Fibonacci state vector $(F_m,F_{m+1})$.  The transition
\[
(x,y)\longmapsto (y,x+y)
\]
has inverse $(x,y)\mapsto (y-x,x)$ modulo $q$, so it is a permutation of the
finite set $(\ZZ/q\ZZ)^2$.  Therefore the orbit of the initial state $(0,1)$
is periodic and returns to $(0,1)$ after some positive number $r$ of steps.
Thus $(F_r,F_{r+1})\equiv(0,1)\pmod q$, in particular $q\mid F_r$.  The
least such positive $r$ exists by well ordering.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:rank-basics}
Let $q,u,v\ge1$ and let $a,b,m\ge1$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $q\mid F_m$ if and only if $\alpha(q)\mid m$.
\item $\gcd(F_a,F_b)=F_{\gcd(a,b)}$.
\item $\alpha(\lcmop(u,v))=\lcmop(\alpha(u),\alpha(v))$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The divisibility criterion and the strong-divisibility identity are classical;
we use them in the forms proved by Wall \cite{Wall1960FibonacciModm} and
Vinson \cite{Vinson1963RankApparition}. The convention $\alpha(1)=1$ makes
the assertion for $q=1$ immediate.

The Fibonacci lcm identity is recorded by Luca--Tron
\cite{LucaTron2015SelfFibonacciDivisors}; Renault gives the corresponding
identity for the more general $(a,b)$-Fibonacci sequences
\cite{Renault2013PeriodRankOrder}. For completeness, an integer $m$ is
divisible by
$\alpha(\lcmop(u,v))$ if and only if $\lcmop(u,v)\mid F_m$, by (i). This is
equivalent to $u\mid F_m$ and $v\mid F_m$, hence to
$\alpha(u)\mid m$ and $\alpha(v)\mid m$, again by (i). Thus the multiples of
$\alpha(\lcmop(u,v))$ are exactly the multiples of
$\lcmop(\alpha(u),\alpha(v))$, and the two positive integers are equal.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact-rank primes and primitive divisors}\label{subsec:primitive}

A prime $p$ is an \emph{exact-rank prime of rank $d$} if $\alpha(p)=d$;
when it is viewed as an atomic prime power, we also call it a \emph{prime
atom}. We reserve \emph{primitive divisor of $F_d$} for the standard
Carmichael--Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier convention: a prime $p$ dividing $F_d$ is
primitive when
\[
 p\nmid 5F_1F_2\cdots F_{d-1}.
\]
Thus every primitive divisor is an exact-rank prime, but the exact-rank prime
$5$ at rank $5$ is excluded from the primitive-divisor terminology because it
divides the discriminant. This distinction will be maintained throughout.

\begin{theorem}[Classical exact-rank existence]\label{thm:primitive-divisors}
For every $n\ge 3$ with $n\notin\{6,12\}$, there exists a prime $p_n$ with
$\alpha(p_n)=n$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $n=3,4,5$ one takes $p_3=2$, $p_4=3$, and $p_5=5$; the last is the
discriminant-related exact-rank exception just described. For $n\ge 7$,
$n\neq 12$, the primitive divisor theorem
\cite{Carmichael1913PrimitiveDivisors,Yabuta2001PrimitiveDivisorFibonacci,%
BiluHanrotVoutier2001PrimitiveDivisorsLucas}
gives a prime dividing $F_n$ but no earlier Fibonacci number. Since
$\alpha(p_n)\mid n$ and $p_n$ divides no $F_d$ with $d<n$, necessarily
$\alpha(p_n)=n$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Counting corollaries}\label{subsec:counting}

The standard M\"obius-inversion formulas for first-appearance counts follow
from Stroi\'nski's $\alpha$-contraction identity
\cite{Stroinski2016AlphaContraction}: for any $f:\NN\to\mathbb{C}$ with
$f(1)=0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stroinski}
\sum_{d\mid F_n}f(d)=\sum_{d\mid n}f_\alpha(d),
\qquad
f_\alpha(d):=\sum_{\alpha(m)=d}f(m).
\end{equation}
Taking $f$ to be the indicator of integers at least $2$, of primes, or of
prime powers, and applying M\"obius inversion, one obtains
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mobius-birth}
A(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(n/d)\,\tau(F_d),
\qquad
A_{\mathrm{pr}}(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(n/d)\,\omega(F_d).
\end{equation}
We regard these as known corollaries; the witness-cover classification below
is independent of them.

\begin{lemma}[Exact-rank prime M\"obius count]\label{lem:primitive-prime-mobius-count}
For $d\ge1$, put
\[
\Pi_\alpha(d):=\{p\text{ prime}:\alpha(p)=d\}.
\]
Then $\Pi_\alpha(d)$ is finite and
\[
\#\Pi_\alpha(d)=\sum_{c\mid d}\mu(d/c)\,\omega(F_c).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For each $d\ge1$, the primes with rank dividing $d$ are exactly the prime
divisors of $F_d$: indeed, for a prime $p$, Proposition~\ref{prop:rank-basics}
gives $p\mid F_d$ if and only if $\alpha(p)\mid d$.  Hence
Proposition~\ref{prop:rank-basics} partitions the distinct prime divisors of
$F_d$ according to their exact ranks $c\mid d$, including the exceptional
primes $2$ and $5$ with ranks $3$ and $5$ respectively; a prime divisor of
$F_c$ can occur earlier only through the condition $\alpha(p)\mid e$ for the
earlier index $e$.
\[
\omega(F_d)=\sum_{c\mid d}\#\Pi_\alpha(c).
\]
The right-hand side is finite because $F_d$ has only finitely many prime
divisors.  Applying M\"obius inversion on the divisor lattice of $d$ gives the
displayed formula.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Atomic notation}\label{subsec:prime-power}

\begin{definition}\label{def:atomic-families}
For a prime power $\theta=p^e$ with $e\ge1$, put
\[
d(\theta):=\alpha(\theta),\qquad
d^-(\theta):=\alpha(p^{e-1}),
\]
where $\alpha(1)=1$. For $d\ge1$ define
\[
\Atom(d):=\{p^e:\ \alpha(p^e)=d,\ \alpha(p^{e-1})<d\}.
\]
Thus $\Atom(d)$ consists of the prime-power factors that are born exactly at
rank $d$.


 succeeded in 224ms:
witness cover $\{13^2\}$ gives the stated strict inclusion.
\end{proof}

The following sharp extremal calculation separates total primitive-prime
mass from the dispersion information retained by the cover polynomial.

\begin{proposition}[Sharp cumulative-data obstruction]
\label{prop:sharp-cumulative-data-obstruction}
Let $V$ be a labelled $k$-set, $k\ge1$, and assign a real weight
$w_S\ge1$ to every nonempty $S\subseteq V$.  Put
\[
 Z_V(w):=\sum_{\mathcal C\in\operatorname{MinCov}(V)}
              \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}w_S,
 \qquad A:=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq V}w_S.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sharp-total-mass-cover-bound}
 Z_V(w)\ge C_k+A-(2^k-1).
\end{equation}
This is best possible for every $A\ge2^k-1$: equality is attained by
\[
 w_V=A-(2^k-2),\qquad w_S=1\quad(S\ne V).
\]
If $k\ge3$, this is the only equality profile.

Consequently, if $n\ge3$ is odd and squarefree and $k=\omega(n)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-cumulative-rank-pure-bound}
 \#\{m\in\mathcal M_n:\mu^2(m)=1\}
 \ge C_k+\omega(F_n)-(2^k-1),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-cumulative-weighted-excess}
 \mathcal W(n)\ge
 1+\frac{\omega(F_n)-(2^k-1)}{C_k}.
\end{equation}
Among all positive weight profiles with the same total
$\sum_{d\mid n,\ d>1}a(d)=\omega(F_n)$, this conclusion is exact.  The
extremizing profile is an abstract information-theoretic model; it is not
asserted to be realized by Fibonacci exact-rank primes.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $w_S=1+x_S$ with $x_S\ge0$.  For every minimal cover $\mathcal C$,
\[
 \prod_{S\in\mathcal C}(1+x_S)
 \ge1+\sum_{S\in\mathcal C}x_S.
\]
Every nonempty $S\subseteq V$ occurs in a minimal cover: use $\{V\}$ when
$S=V$, and otherwise use
$\{S\}\cup\{\{v\}:v\in V\setminus S\}$.  Summing the preceding inequality
over all minimal covers therefore gives
\[
 Z_V(w)\ge C_k+\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq V}x_S
 =C_k+A-(2^k-1).
\]

 succeeded in 214ms:

\begin{proof}
For the first assertion, intersect the density-one set in
Definition~\ref{hyp:normal-window} with the Hardy--Ramanujan normal-order set.
Then the error $\omega(n)\log R(n)+O(\omega(n))$ in
\eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order} is $o(\omega(n)^2)$. For the second,
sum the upper bound in \eqref{eq:private-cover-entropy-order}, use
Definition~\ref{hyp:mean-window}, \eqref{eq:omega-second-moment}, and
$\sum_{n\le x}\omega(n)=O(x\log\log x)$, and compare with the unconditional
lower mean bound \eqref{eq:mean-logarithmic-growth}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Exact residual arithmetic interface]
\label{rem:exact-average-open-interface}
Supplementary Corollary~\ref{supp-cor:theta-refined-connected-entropy} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:minimal-generator-growth} determine the contribution of the
multiplicity-free minimal-cover model used here: $\log2/4$ is its exact
quadratic coefficient, the fixed-choice rank-pure sector supplies its
theta-refined lower terms, and the private-coordinate upper bound retains the
term $\omega(n)\log R(n)$. Proposition~
\ref{prop:atomic-family-ladder-slots} gives the rank-window description for
every support size:
prime-atom families are finite disjoint unions of the rank
classes $\Pi_\alpha(d)$, while ladder families are singletons. Thus
Hypotheses \textup{(H1)} and \textup{(H2)} in Definitions~
\ref{hyp:normal-window} and \ref{hyp:mean-window} ask precisely for sufficient
upper distributional control of
exact-rank prime multiplicities in those rank windows.

The pointwise estimate \eqref{eq:pointwise-exact-rank-bound} proves only the
consequence obtained by combining the fibotomic size identity with the
divisibility of the exact-rank radical and the congruences
$p\equiv\pm1\pmod d$. Supplementary Remark~
\ref{supp-rem:cumulative-rank-estimates-insufficient} shows that even its
Euler-factor scale does not by itself imply either maximum-window hypothesis. The
manuscript does not prove that these inputs exhaust the information available
from fibotomic arithmetic, or that
\eqref{eq:fibotomic-rank-entropy} is optimal within any information class. Such
an optimality theorem would require, at minimum, a precise axiom class of
admissible information and a realizable extremal model satisfying those
axioms. Neither is supplied here. Whether stronger unconditional dispersion or
concentration consequences follow from fibotomic arithmetic is therefore an
open interface.

The closest distribution theorems do not close this interface.  In the
notation of a nondegenerate Lucas sequence $U$ with nonsquare discriminant
$\Delta$ and squarefree discriminant part $\Delta_0$, Sanna's Theorem~1.1
\cite{Sanna2022RankDivisibility} gives, unconditionally, an asymptotic formula
for
\[
 \#\{p\le x:d\mid\rho_U(p)\}
\]
when $d$ is odd, with $3\nmid d$ if $\Delta_0=-3$, and
$x>\exp(Be^{8\omega(d)}d^8)$.  The condition counted is
$d\mid\rho_U(p)$, not the exact-rank condition $\rho_U(p)=d$; for example,
$3\mid\alpha(17)=9$ although $\alpha(17)\ne3$.  Sanna's Theorem~1.1 on the
index of appearance \cite{Sanna2024IndexAppearance} instead assumes GRH and,
for a fixed positive integer $t$, counts primes having
$\iota_U(p)=t$ uniformly for $x>t^3$. Cera Da Concei\c c\~ao removes the
restrictions in the fixed rank-divisibility density problem and gives
explicit densities for all Lucas sequences and fixed $d$
\cite{CeraDaConceicao2026PrimeDensities}; the condition remains
$d\mid\rho_U(p)$ and still does not isolate a growing exact fiber. In the
Fibonacci case,
$\alpha(p)=d$ and $\iota_F(p)=t$ imply
\[
 p=td+\left(\frac5p\right)=td\pm1.
\]
Taking $x$ on the scale of this candidate prime restricts that theorem to
$t^2\ll d$, and its cumulative fixed-index count still does not isolate the
single rank $d$. Thus none of these results controls the growing exact fibers
$\Pi_\alpha(d)$ required in \eqref{eq:visible-single-rank-interface}.

The primitive-divisor theorems of Carmichael and Bilu--Hanrot--Voutier give
existence in the Fibonacci setting. Granville proves an odd-multiplicity
primitive-factor theorem for a class of second-order Lucas recurrences with
$c\equiv2\pmod4$ and explicitly leaves the odd-$b,c$ case, including the
Fibonacci recurrence $b=c=1$, outside that theorem
\cite{Granville2012PrimitivePrimeFactors}. Thus it is a neighboring
Lucas-sequence result, not a Fibonacci multiplicity input here. Kiss treats
related Lucas-sequence primitive-divisor questions
\cite{Kiss1988PrimitiveDivisors}. Hong proves that, for each fixed positive
integer $\kappa$ and all sufficiently large $n$ with an explicit threshold,
$F_n$ has a primitive divisor at least $(\kappa+1)n-1$
\cite{Hong2025BigPrimitiveDivisors}. These results concern existence, parity of
one primitive factor's multiplicity in the stated Lucas class, or the size of
one Fibonacci primitive divisor; they do not provide the required average
dispersion or critical concentration estimates for Fibonacci exact-rank prime
multiplicities in visible divisor windows. The
Hardy--Ramanujan and
